@@2061309 <p> From decisions on pipelines , investment in LNG , what to spend and what to cut , it 's shaping up to be a landmark year for Canada 's oil and gas industry . Keep reading . <p> Kinder Morgan Canada was the first pipeline company to give it a try a couple of years ago . <p> It ditched the old rulebook , which for decades prescribed convincing a small number of regulators about projects ' largely technical and economic merits . <p> And it sketched a new way that involves a lot of talking , handholding , empathizing , addressing myths and fears , with a large number of impacted or just interested people . <p> It is time consuming and costly . Eventually , it will lead to higher costs and lower competitiveness . <p> But Ian Anderson , president of Kinder Morgan Canada , said it 's working -- people feel the company has been open and considerate of their views , opinions are better-informed and there is greater awareness about the benefits of building pipelines . <p> I think we have demonstrated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ others are looking at -- whether it 's other companies or governments <p> Other pipeline companies and governments are adopting the lessons learned and spending more time than ever building bridges before they get on with building pipelines . <p> " As small as Kinder Morgan is in Canada , we are influencing the hearts and minds of a broader community , " Mr. Anderson said in an interview . " I think we have demonstrated an approach , a style , a practice , that others are looking at -- whether it 's other companies or governments -- and are appreciative of it , and hopefully learning something as we learn from others along the way . " <p> Indeed , competitor Enbridge Inc. applied the approach to its proposal to reverse Line 9 in Ontario and Quebec . TransCanada Corp . went all out on its Energy East pipeline plan . Douglas Eyford , the Prime Minister 's envoy on West Coast energy infrastructure , recommended the new way in his Dec. 5 report to improve aboriginal benefits . <p> Joe Oliver , Canada 's natural resources minister , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on trust is the key to creating momentum and I am personally committed to making this happen , " he said . <p> Kinder Morgan Canada , a subsidiary of Houston-based Kinder Morgan Energy Partners , L.P. , is highly motivated . It applied to the National Energy Board Dec. 16 for regulatory approval of a $5.4-billion expansion of its TransMountain oil pipeline from Edmonton to Vancouver from about 300,000 barrels a day to 890,000 barrels a day . The filing came after 18 months of extensive consultations with landowners , aboriginal groups , and other parties along the pipeline route . <p> The company expects a 15-month review and decision timeframe . <p> When Kinder Morgan Canada applied for its last expansion of the same pipeline in 2008 , it faced so little opposition it completed its regulatory hearing within a week . The Aseniwuche Winewak and Simpcw First Nations and the Alberta Metis Zone IV community were glad for the jobs and contracts . The Town of Jasper and the Village of Valemount thanked Kinder Morgan Canada for the opportunity . <p> But those were the good old days @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tied up in knots . First Nations and environmental organizations are campaigning against more tanker traffic , pipeline expansions and Alberta 's oil sands . Views are rigid . Insults are flying . <p> A recent survey of 300 Canadian aboriginals by the University of Calgary 's School of Public Policy found that about one-third have zero trust in oil and gas companies , energy executives , and the Canadian Association of Petroleum Producers , the Canadian oil industry 's association . <p> Mr. Anderson , who two years ago cleared his schedule to focus on building the TransMountain expansion , is dedicating the largest part of his time -- 20% to 30% -- to engaging with aboriginals through direct discussions with chiefs and councils in reserves , reviewing correspondence and exchanging information . The company is in touch with 103 aboriginal communities impacted by the project and is in deep conversations with 35 to 40 . <p> " We have really tried to walk into First Nations communities with no agenda , with no prescribed list , with no set approach and we let the relationship form and develop trust @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from there , which takes time , but we are investing the time . " <p> First Nations tend to have unique concerns , based on their history , their experiences with commerce , their desire for development opportunities , Mr. Anderson said . <p> The company is also talking to politicians at all levels -- municipal , provincial , federal -- so there are clear expectations on both sides . It has spent a lot of time with the B.C. government to understand its five conditions of moving oil through the province -- regulatory approval , world-leading response to marine and land spills , opportunities for aboriginals , a fair share of benefits for B.C. <p> Kinder Morgan also calls on environmentalists , although they 've been a lower priority so far . Mr. Anderson said their views tend to be about broader issues that are well-understood -- climate change , the oil sands , environmental protection . <p> But another lesson has also been learned in case building trust does n't yield the desired results : bulk up on rail . Like the rest of the Canadian energy sector @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on railways . On Dec. 20 . , Kinder Morgan and Imperial Oil Ltd. announced construction of a crude oil rail loading facility in Strathcona Country , near Edmonton , to provide a " strategic bridge " to TransMountain 's expansion .

@@2062643 <h> ' Worst ' of climate predictions are the most likely : new study <p> New research predicts that global temperatures will likely rise at least 4 ? C by 2100 and potentially more than 8 ? C by 2200 if carbon dioxide emissions are not reduced and serious actions by governments and society are not taken . ( Photo : LiveScience.com ) <p> New research by a team of scientists looking at the impact of cloud behavior on planetary climate change says that " the worst " and " catastrophic " predictions offered by previous studies on the rate of global warming this century are much more likely than the less severe scenarios offered by others . <p> According to Dr. Steven Sherwood , lead author of the study and a specialist in climate and cloud formations at the University of New South Wales in Australia , cloud patterns in an increasingly warming world are likely to exacerbate global temperature increases overall , not mitigate warming as some models have suggested . <p> " This study breaks new ground twice , " Sherwood explained in an interview @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ controlling the cloud changes and second by strongly discounting the lowest estimates of future global warming in favor of the higher and more damaging estimates . " <p> The research predicts that global temperatures will likely rise at least 4 ? C by 2100 and potentially more than 8 ? C by 2200 if carbon dioxide emissions are not reduced and serious actions by governments and society are not taken . <p> Such drastic increases , Sherwood told the Guardian would be " catastrophic rather than simply dangerous . " <p> Specifically , the study looked at how already warming temperatures impact the ability of clouds to form at high altitudes , because that ability can greatly impact the way in which large clouds help the planet reflect or absorb the sun 's heat . One of the long-acknowledged shortcomings of other climate computer models -- which drive much of the work of climate change predictions -- is the difficulty of accurately projecting cloud behavior under complex , future conditions . <p> By tackling that problem , say Sherwood and his colleagues , their research " cracks open one of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , an adjunct professor at Australian National University and a member of the Climate Council , said the paper was ' ' right out on the forefront of the sort of research we need to do on clouds ' ' . <p> ' ' The more we get research like from Professor Sherwood and his group , the more confidence we 'll have in being able to say where in that range the temperature increase is likely to fall . ' ' <p> The CO2 level of the atmosphere in 2012 was 393 parts per million , or 41 per cent higher than in pre-industrial times , the World Meteorological Organisation said . The level is rising at an accelerating rate - more than two parts per million a year - as humans burn more fossil fuels and cut down forests . <p> Global temperatures have risen about 0.8 degrees since about 1880 , with system lags and air pollution reflecting sunlight partly explaining why the rise has not been higher . ' ' We 've been hoping for the best and not planning for the worst , ' ' Professor @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ looking like the best is not very likely . ' ' <p> In 2012 , a study by the World Bank -- an institution not typically known for sounding the alarm over industrial , human-caused global warming -- also said that a 4 ? C temperature rise was not only possible , but likely if humanity did not change course . According to Kim Yong Kim , the bank 's president , the resulting impacts of a planet that warm would likely " make the world our children inherit a completely different world than we are living in today . "
